Why So Many Men Are Unraveling
2026-09-04

Why So Many Men Are Unraveling

The Atlantic | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
Jon Ronson had never really thought about the term masculinity until a strange series of events led him down a rabbit hole involving castles, cage fights, and an actor playing a horse. Host Charlie Warzel talks to Ronson about the disinhibited men who are looking for purpose in all the wrong places. Subscribe here: Apple Podcasts | Spotify | YouTube The following is a transcript of the episode: Jon Ronson: So I think, beginning with deindustrialization, the death of factories, office life eroding, men realizing that certain privileges they had they don’t quite have the same way anymore—feeling kind of tossed around in a sea of conflicting ideologies about what being a man actually means. Should you be more stoical? Should you be more vulnerable? You know, all of these men’s-health influencers giving these differing, contrasting ideas. And so this is a huge societal shift in many different ways. And I think that’s what’s happening. You’ve got men responding to these changes by bowing out, and then you’ve got these other men who are responding by spiraling. Charlie Warzel: I’m Charlie Warzel, and this is Galaxy Brain, and today we are headed down a rabbit hole that includes ominous castles, public shaming, YouTube, White House cage fighting, all to answer one of life’s burning questions. Why is it that so many men are unraveling these days? Is this part of a changing economy? Part of a bad job market? Is it fallout from the pandemic? Or, is it part of something bigger? A societal failure of some sort, or an outgrowth of what some people have called the masculinity crisis? Now those words, masculinity crisis, can cause some people to roll their eyes. Men, of course, continue to enjoy all kinds of societal privilege, and gender inequities abound. But what is clear, I think, if you spent any time engaging with modern politics and culture, is that over the last few decades there has been a very real, if hard to define, debate over what it means to be a man. Jobs and gender roles—they’ve changed, and culture along with it. Much of it for the better. But that change has also created some uncertainty for the men who have been used to being at the center of every household, every office, every institution, marketing campaign, and political decision. And it’s in that uncertainty that all kinds of voices, pickup artists, politicians, incels on 4chan, Andrew Tate, Jordan Peterson—you name it—they’ve all jumped in to offer these competing visions of masculinity. Now, some of this has taken the form of toxic, misogynistic, manosphere bullshit. Some of it is just positive or harmless—“dudes rock” kind of stuff. And so many of these lessons, they end up being taught or absorbed, for better or for worse, in online communities and communicated by people who may or may not have ulterior motives. So what happens to regular men in this bizarre online stew that we’re all in? My guest today, the writer and podcaster Jon Ronson, explores that in his new book, The Castle. Ronson, to be clear, isn’t really writing about the manosphere or the high-profile grifters or the wild extremes. Instead, Ronson focuses on ordinary people who tend to find themselves in extraordinary situations. For the past three years, Ronson has been reporting on what happens to men when institutions die, when technology lets a million influencers bloom, and culture changes rapidly. What I find so useful about Ronson’s work is that it is curious and is funny, but it’s not overly ideological. The Castle is written partly as a mystery and partly as a work of anthropology. For the last decade, Ronson and I have crossed paths on stories numerous times. Like me, his work is always trying to understand what pushes people to believe strange or awful things, and all the ways that technology warps culture, which in turn warps real human beings. So—are men okay? Has the internet driven us all insane? It turns out this is a very complicated question. And Jon Ronson joins me now to talk about it all. Warzel: Jon Ronson, welcome to Galaxy Brain. Ronson: Charlie, it’s a delight to be here. Thank you. Warzel: So your newest book, the reason you are talking to me right now—it’s called The Castle, and you’ve written it in such a propulsive, narrative way. But the story is also unique, I think, in that it found you. So tell me a bit about the castle and how it entered your life in what is clearly an extremely personal way. Ronson: Yes. I am asleep, and I wake up, and there’s a series of texts from my son, who was in his mid-20s at the time. And so I read the last text first, and the last text says something like: It’s okay; that was completely fucked, but we got out, and we’re safe now. Or something along those lines. Ronson: So I frantically read the other texts. And what I gather is that him and three friends have been invited to a party being held by a stranger that one of them had met on Tinder. So it’s like three hours’ drive outside of New York City, into the Connecticut countryside. And when they turn up, this man’s house is a vast, newly built Gothic castle, which is ostentatious even by Gothic-castle standards. It has way too many turrets. And so they tentatively cross the drawbridge over the moat and enter to discover that there is no party. They’ve been lured there for some mysterious other reason. So the castle owner comes out, and they say, “I’m sorry, but where’s the party?” And he said, “No, I’ve invited you here to take part in my reality show.” So they said, “What’s the name of your reality show?” And he said, “It’s called 101 Princesses.” They asked what the core concept of 101 Princesses was, and he said, “I’m the lord. I’m looking for my queen, and I’ve got 101 princesses to choose from.” Filming began, and it became very clear, very quickly, that this wasn’t a real reality show. He had another agenda. Anyway, that’s basically where their story ends. But I then went to the castle. I was so intrigued by what the hell was going on in there. I went to the castle, and a strange and increasingly ominous cat-and-mouse game begins between me and the castle owner. What I thought was the story was that—This guy is trying to be Hugh Hefner. He’s trying to get his, like, you know, princesses in his castle, like the Playboy Mansion. He’s trying to be Hugh Hefner at what is the worst moment in human history to try and be Hugh Hefner. You can’t lord it over your princesses and your castle like it’s the 1970s anymore. Throughout the book, I return to the castle to try and find out what the hell is going on. And what I learned gets increasingly dark and extraordinary. But I also tell this wider story of unraveling men failing to adjust to changing times. Warzel: So tell me a little bit. The castle owner’s name, I believe, it’s Chris Mark, right? Is the name of the castle owner. But he is a very particular type of character. Tell me a little bit about him, and what immediately caught your eye about this guy. Ronson: Yeah, well he’s the great-grandson of a Gilded Age steel tycoon. That’s where his money comes from. His great-grandfather, Clayton Mark, made a vast amount of money manufacturing steel, and then the money is filtered down from generation to generation. And Chris has used his portion of the money to build this giant castle and try and create this kind of fantasy bubble inside of his castle, where he could be the lord. And he invites all of these women. But he doesn’t handle my sudden intrusion into his life very well at all. And by the way, it’s not an intrusion. I told him I was a journalist, and he invited me over. But after I leave the cast, so I start to get these ominous messages from him. First, legal threats. Threats that he had a team of investigators investigating everything about my life. And then he told me that he had two murderers on his payroll, called Carlos and Brandon. And if I ever wrote anything negative about the castle, Carlos and Brandon would be very angry, and I didn’t want to make them angry. They’ve only just got out of prison. Clearly this is a difficult situation I’m now in. Warzel: Hey, guys—it’s Charlie here. A quick interjection: After we recorded this interview with Jon Ronson, Chris Mark—the central character of the book—spoke with The Guardian and pushed back on some of the assertions in The Castle. He told The Guardian that he’d been presented in “a profoundly misleading manner.” Mark denied deliberately deceiving people into coming to the castle, adding that “the gathering was related to a proposed creative project that also had a genuine charitable purpose.” He added: “I strongly dispute any characterization suggesting that I groomed, conditioned, or manipulated women into sexual activity.” And he denied that the text messages referencing Carlos and Brandon in any way constituted a genuine threat: “I did not intend to instruct anyone to physically harm Jon Ronson.” With all that said, back to the interview. Warzel: Was that a moment for you? Because it feels like it in the book, where you haven’t decided to make this your next big project. And yet the amount of protestation from him, the intensity of that—is that the moment when you say, Okay, there’s something real here? Like, I need to devote a lot more time to this, because clearly anyone who’s willing to speak this way to me—there has to be something bigger than just an eccentric man in a castle here. Ronson: Yeah; there was definitely an element of that. That was also, you know—but also it was kind of frightening to get these threats. And I remember times in the past when I’ve done stories which were genuinely frightening. And on each of those occasions, I realized that doing something that’s completely out of my comfort zone, something I would never do if I wasn’t a journalist, quite often the stories that last for years and years are the stories that I’m most proud of. They’re often the most extraordinary stories. And this one truly turned out to be one of those. Warzel: Yeah, that’s exactly where I want to go here. Because the story about Mark becomes this through line that animates this bigger story about—I mean, I guess to put it frankly, it’s: What the hell is going on with men in this era? Right? And there’s this personal element that you refer to a couple of times. In this sort of paralyzing fear in your own life, in your own career, of any kind of stagnation, right? Being a writer when you don’t have the idea; you know, you feel purposeless and useless. Have you felt in your own life a masculinity crisis inside of you, that like allowed you to connect with a lot of the people in this story, in this way? This unraveling feeling. Is that something that’s always kind of been a factor in your life and your work? Ronson: Well, I would never have put it in terms of the word masculinity. I don’t think, until I started writing this book, I ever said the word masculinity out loud. That’s how little I identify with masculinity. I discovered a statistic writing the book that 10 percent of people feel the same way I do—that we just don’t identify as strongly masculine or feminine. So we’re really in the minority. But this desire for purpose, this need to be productive. I once asked Randy Newman this question: Why do you write songs? And he said, It’s how I judge myself, and how I feel better. And that always stayed with me. Because that’s why I am a writer; it’s how I judge myself and how I feel better. And I thought—That’s relevant. Because, you know, we’re living in a time where there’s 6.7 million American men who aren’t only unemployed; they’re not even looking for work. They’ve just bowed out. And so, you know, if purpose is that important to me, what happens to somebody when purpose vanishes? And that was something I wanted to explore. Warzel: I wanted to find it a little more—like, what would you argue is happening to these men, who are not in the workforce? Like, what is happening here? Ronson: I think, you know, toward the end of researching this, I came across Émile Durkheim’s work on suicide. And he invented this term called anomie, which is that when society abruptly changes, it turns people haywire. And often that manifests as somebody folding in on themselves, withdrawing from the world. A good example is the fall of the Soviet Union. Even people who hated living under Soviet rule would find themselves, after it fell, sitting on the pavement in despair, with their head in their hands, because society’s rules have changed and they didn’t know what to do. And then, some people respond not by folding in but by lashing out in these unrestrained, disinhibited ways. So I think a form of anomie is what has taken over, beginning with deindustrialization, the death of factories, office life eroding, men realizing that certain privileges they had they don’t quite have the same weight anymore—feeling kind of tossed around in a sea of conflicting ideologies about what being a man actually means. Should you be more stoical? Should you be more vulnerable? You know, all of these men’s-health influencers giving these differing, contrasting ideas. And then a lot of men who were in this situation become influencers; they go onto YouTube and become content creators. And so this is a huge societal shift in many different ways. And I think that’s what’s happening. You’ve got men responding to these changes by bowing out, and then you’ve got these other men who are responding by spiraling. Warzel: So you do this great job of mapping all this. But there’s a point in the book, and I want to tease this out more, where you acknowledge sort of as you’re as you’re moving on from Mark and the castle to make this a broader subject of the book. You acknowledge that this is a hard thing to talk about, right? Because it can veer into sounding like you’re setting up a misogynistic argument. So I’m curious—like what would you say to somebody who hears the premise of The Castle and just goes, Please, not another book trying to drum up pity for the least-subjugated people on Earth—you know, primarily white men. What do you think it is that these books, or the discourse around this issue of men—like, what do you think they haven’t put their finger on yet? What is the dissatisfaction that you see in that genre? Ronson: Well, I think a lot of the problem is that people, you know, a lot of the people who approach this subject are ideological. And I’m not. I have no agenda whatsoever. I think we’re living in this sort of—I picture an astronaut getting cut loose from their craft and tumbling into the blackness of space. I think all of these things: the death of institutions, the false promise of YouTube as a kind of post-institution utopia. Where people, you know, where a man can thrive, like a wide-open country where a man can thrive through perseverance and grit as an influencer. But of course, that’s a false promise. Personally speaking, the thing that interests me the most is what happens when institutions die. Very often, they die for good reason. However, I think that does something to people’s inner lives—this lack of purpose and so on. And I’m not sure that that has properly been written about in this way. And I think that’s a kind of contribution that this book makes. Warzel: You acknowledge in the book that in some ways these institutions have failed, but that what can replace them—in the sense of, you know, YouTube in this case—is sometimes worse. And how much would you say the behaviors of the men in this book—that this problem of despair, resentment, and idleness—is due to that collapse of faith, and also the failure of some of these institutions? Ronson: Yeah, I think that’s quite a big part of it. Also, another world I look at in this book is the world of the... it’s a smaller part of the book, but I think a very relevant one. It’s the world of the sort of anti-woke influencer. The kind of man who’ll say, The only reason why I haven’t got ahead in life is because I’m a white man, and now we’re being prejudiced against. And it hurts. And so on. And then I dig in, and so they become anti-woke influencers, and they realize that the more extreme—the more they present themselves in a kind of victim narrative—the more money they make. And so they double down on this. And I really dig into that and look at when they have a point, and when they don’t have a point. When they’re, you know, exaggerating or manipulating their stories for this kind of nefarious, anti-woke ideological ends. So that’s another thing that the book looks at. I’ve got one small section of the book where I look at doofus men in TV advertising. It used to always be, in the old days, women were the figures of mockery in adverts. And now that’s shifted, and men are the figures of mockery. Warzel: What is the reason for that? The shift? Ronson: Well, I think partly it just became passé to disparage women in TV ads. Also women control most household-spending habits. And so that’s part of the reason why they stopped making women the sort of butt of the jokes. Ronson: Yeah, exactly. But then advertising, you know—there’s such a paucity of imagination. They think, Well, we’ll just make somebody else the idiots. If it’s not women, it’s going to have to be men. So I’ve watched a kind of, you know, orgy of male buffoonery to research that part of the book. Men falling off cliffs; men leaving their babies on restaurant conveyor belts; men being terrible at sex, you know. And I actually interviewed one of the actors who plays Doofus in Adverts. It made me laugh. He said he did an ad for—what are the insoles? Warzel: Dr. Scholl’s, the foot pads? Yeah. Ronson: Yeah; he did an advert for them where he said that he’s walking to work and he puts on a pair of insoles, and they’re so comfortable he starts galloping like a horse. And he had to be a bit doofusy for that. So I pointed out to him that 50 years ago, you had the Marlboro Man being stoical and masculine on a horse, and now he has to be the horse. And he pointed out that, yeah—but who do you identify with more? The doofus or the Marlboro Man? And I have to admit that I identify more with the doofus. Warzel: And so it seems like, I mean, is someone listening to this might say, you know, where is the through line there? Warzel: But it’s a kind of a death-by-a-thousand-cuts thing, right? Like, especially if you are someone who feels this subjugation, right? Rightly or wrongly, that if you start looking for it, you can see it in so many different places. And seeing it in so many different places becomes—Okay now, I’m under assault. Now I’m under attack. There are no positive, strong role models. Things like that. There’s no Marlboro Man, whatever. And then it seems, like, to put the institutions and the alternative influencers that come in to take the place of that—that you have these people creating their own versions of that. Of something to aspire to. And you have this line that I’d love for you to expound on more. Where you say: “As institutions crumble, narcissists thrive.” Can you tease that out a little more for me? Ronson: We declared war on institutions. That’s the word, you know, often for very good reasons. Priests. I don’t need to go through the list of all the terrible things that institutions have done. But media gatekeepers kept out the voices they found unpalatable. Hospitals screwed, or health insurance companies screwed us with ridiculous and frightening and baffling fees. So there’s a long list of institutional malfeasance. However, at their best, institutions are a consensus. They’re coming together. As I quoted Yuval Levin, who says institutions teach us that we have duties and responsibilities. And we have, you know, subordinates; we have superiors. Institutions at their best remind us that we have a place in the world. Whereas a narcissist is just some guy who fills that gap with grand histrionic rage and proclamations. So I guess when institutions die, we lose our sense of place in the world. And so we listen to these grandiose, histrionic narcissistic content creators instead. Yeah. And it leads us to bad places. One thing in this book is how much fakery there is. You’ve got man after man in this book, living fake lives. So because when you’re unmoored from reality, you create these fake lives. And that manifests in many different ways in the book. In The Castle, you’ve got Chris Mark with his fake party and his fake reality show. You’ve got these anti-woke influencers who are reimagining the things that have happened in their past to create this sort of you know, fake life for themselves. And who will spout all of this extreme discourse and not even mean it? They have fake venomous beliefs. There’s fakery over and over again in this book. But when you build your house on sand, when you create a fake life, it could do something to you psychologically. And I think that’s what I’m really trying to dig into in those parts of the book. And what does that do not only to you, but to your loved ones? Can I briefly go back to something that happened at the UFC—at the cage fight at the White House? Warzel: Please. Tell me more about the cage fight, yes. Ronson: Okay. Yeah, there were a number of moments that week at the White House where men were creating a world where “you have to bend to my rules.” Like: I’m the boss; you have to; what I say goes; you have to bend to it. And I saw several occasions when reality snapped back. And one occasion was when I was interviewing this fighter called Sean O’Malley, who’s like a kind of bit of a UFC heartthrob. And he would always boast in podcast interviews about his open marriage. And it’s a one-sided open marriage. He’s allowed to have sex with other women, but his wife, Danya, isn’t allowed to have sex with other men. And the way he would describe it on podcasts is, well—Andrew Tate put it well, I have status. I have status. I can do what I want. So when I interviewed him at the White House, I said, “You know, you have an open marriage. Surely that doesn’t work.” And he said, “Well, it worked until it didn’t.” And it turns out that his open marriage is over. His wife said to him, like, no. His marriage is still going, but the openness of it is now over. And he looked so baffled at this unfortunate change in the circumstances where he wasn’t allowed to do that anymore. And I noticed over and over again these little moments when men suddenly realized they can’t do that shit anymore. And it made me wonder whether the pendulum is beginning to swing back a little bit. Warzel: So there is a section in your book that stopped me cold. I’ve never experienced anything like this before. Ronson: My god, I’m slightly trepidatious about what you’re about to say. Warzel: One of the book’s subjects is a man named Greg Chism. And can you just briefly tell me the story of Greg Chism? Ronson: You wrote about Greg Chism right? Warzel: I didn’t realize exactly that I had, but I have typed those words in a fashion that actually seems to have had a pretty large impact on this man’s life. Ronson: That’s right. Wow, okay. Greg Chism was well within the bracket of somebody most likely to die a death of despair. He came from a crumbling, Rust Belt town—Granite City, Illinois. He was destined to work at the factory, but the factories all closed down. Then when he’s 18 years old, he has a heart condition that he doesn’t realize, until he’s working in Taco Bell one night and he collapses unconscious. And ends up with multiple open-heart surgeries and a $200,000 medical bill. And then he marries somebody, has two kids, and then his wife leaves. So he’s a single father with two kids in this dire situation. Has no idea what to do with his life. But he watches a YouTuber who’s a guy called Durianrider, a fruitarian, cyclist YouTuber. Ronson: Durianrider says: Be a YouTuber; tell your story. You must have a story. So Greg starts to tell his story. His first YouTube thing is adult braces. He’s got terribly overlapping teeth because he couldn’t afford to get his teeth fixed as a kid. So he borrows $6,000 and does an adult-braces journey. Ronson: People start following along, you know: We’re so proud of you. Then somebody says, Why have you got lawn mowers? He says, Well, because I do lawn care. And they’re like, Bro, be a lawn-care influencer. And he’s like, Bro, nobody wants a lawn-care influencer. They go, No, do a lawn-care vlog. So he becomes ike this kind of Zorro of lawn-care chores. He’ll like, turn up at somewhere. He’ll film himself doing Good Samaritan lawn work, and, you know, clean the edges—and then vanish again into the night. Well, day; you can’t really mow at night. And that’s where he and Zorro differ. Then he does lawn-care ASMR, where people just watch him mowing back and forward. And he is having the time of his life. He goes to a lawn-care convention in Kentucky, and he’s feted. He’s like the lawn-care GOAT. Ronson: So then, something bad starts to happen. So he has this sister channel just called Greg Chism, where he’ll put up home videos. Him and his kids running up and down the Walmart aisle with light sabers or whatever. Had like a few hundred subscribers. But he discovers one day that this one video on the Greg Chism channel called “Unboxing Barbie Cruise Ship”—where his daughters open up a gift and put the boxes on their head and run around. It gets like millions of views. The kids are really adorable; they’re sweet. And suddenly he’s got a video with 10 million views. So YouTube phoned him, and they’re like, Do more of that. So he goes to like YouTube school—this kind of online school where he’s told to follow the algorithms. You know, this is the original sin, I guess, of Greg Chism’s story. So one day he’s watching this vlog of a teenage girl reminiscing about what it’s like to be a baby. She’s like, I used to like this toy, but now I don’t. And Greg’s daughters are laughing along at this. And Greg thinks: I could do this and take it further. I could get, you know, somebody to put a magic wand on my daughter, and she magically turns into a baby. Yeah. So that’s what he does. He puts a, you know, pacifier in her mouth and has her do this exaggerated baby talk. “Goo, poop.” And it’s a massive hit. Then the next one, he gets his second daughter to dress up as a baby. So now it’s “Two Bad Babies.” By now, “Bad Baby” is so popular that there’s like Ukrainians and Vietnamese people assiduously re-creating everything that Greg does the next day. Like, the same camera angles, the same scripts, like this kind of knock-off Fendi bag she found on the side of the road. And then he realizes he has to, like, constantly up it. Because that’s what his fans and what the algorithm is telling him to. And so he becomes the third baby. So you’ve suddenly got these bizarre videos of him and his two daughters dressed as babies crawling around the house. Warzel: And so—and this is where I wanna stop you, because this is where I come in. I, in 2017, was doing a bunch of reporting on YouTube and the platform, and a bunch of really objectionable stuff on there. And this channel with the baby stuff was also named Toy Freaks, because it started with the unboxing video. Ronson: Yes; YouTube told him that Greg Chism wasn’t a good enough name for his channel, so he changed it to Toy Freaks. Warzel: Right. And one of the bigger stories that I broke in my time at BuzzFeed was about this this broad, child-exploitation-content stuff that was happening. Where men were making these videos using children, and some of them were extremely disturbing. Some of them were disturbing for other reasons. And Greg’s seems to be, like, right—it was one of the larger examples of this, because some of those videos were so, so popular, getting hundreds and hundreds of millions of views. But there was also this cottage industry of, as you said, knockoffs. But also even weirder ones, that were actually, you know, duct-taping children to walls. Like very, very inappropriate stuff. Our reporting got YouTube to take his channel down. And you—when I saw my own reporting cited in it, I was taken aback. Because not only did you have the story behind the story that I had written years ago, but also the fallout from it. And again, to be clear, these videos were not—they found no, you know, foul play with his kids, or anything terrible in that sense. They were just really weird. And they were also being viewed—one thing that my reporting showed—by a lot of creeps, right. Who were kind of getting off to this idea of, you know, these girls in these strange situations and adult babies and all that. But it had this profound effect on his life. You know, it had him become more alienated. And, for me, it was actually—it’s just a very jarring and profound thing. Because, you know, you’re doing this kind of journalism, this accountability work. You know: YouTube’s not moderating their platform. In fact, what you show is that YouTube was encouraging this type of behavior, right? No, you need a third baby. You need to change the thing. Take this video down, because the algorithm finds it, you know, displeasing. And go with something else. And I am curious: Do you see Chism just bluntly as a victim of the algorithm? Ronson: No. I think, you know, around the same time I was writing columns for The Guardian about my own domestic life. Where I was telling these, like, funny stories about my family and so on. And I kind of sorely regret it now. I mean, they were perfectly harmless and nothing like the kind of content that we’re talking about, the Elsagate content. Warzel: Yeah. You didn’t dress as an adult baby. Ronson: No. But even so, I regret doing it. So in a way, sort of empathized for that reason. Like, I made the same mistake as he did. You know, he made his choices, but he did also cede his life to the algorithm, too. What I really like about the story, by the way, is, you came to the story like the whole world did. At the end of the story, here’s a guy dressed as a baby with his two daughters dressed as babies. Your very rational thought would have been, what the fuck? When you, my British editor said, this is the frog in slowly boiling water. When you’re with Greg from the beginning of his story, all of his decisions make a kind of sense. You wouldn’t necessarily want to do it yourself, but it’s an extreme example. But maybe it’s an extreme example of something that a hell of a lot of people are now experiencing—which is that we think that YouTube is this wide-open country where you can just thrive as a content creator. But what you maybe don’t realize is that you’re actually being propelled by YouTube’s incentive structure. So you may end up doing things; you may end up becoming more and more anti-woke, or you may end up dressing up as a baby. And there’s probably a million different versions of that, where just the sort of subtle incentive structures of the algorithms, the fans, the internet itself, propels you into more and more unwise decision making. So I guess that’s what I’m exploring in that story. Warzel: Yeah, I think you see it in so many places. I mean, there are a few people who participated in the January 6th riots at the Capitol—one of which, this figure who goes by Baked Alaska, who was a at one point a BuzzFeed employee, right? And was someone who would do increasingly ridiculous things for views, for likes, for whatever. And here’s a way in which a series of those types of algorithmically flavored decisions that they have to obviously take full accountability for, but led you to, you know, sitting at Nancy Pelosi’s desk in the Capitol on January 6. It’s like how, you know—record scratch, freeze frame, “How did I get here?” But I’m so curious: What do you see as technology’s role in this unraveling as it happens with men in this moment? Because the book veers in and out of it all the time. It’s not just him—like Chris Mark, for example, in some of your reporting, is this rich, alienated man with this castle who’s also—not to spoil too much—in like a sycophantic, problematic relationship with ChatGPT, right? So where do you see technology’s role in this, enabling all this? Ronson: Wildly culpable, is their role. In many different ways. And let’s take ChatGPT as an example. In the audiobook of The Castle, I’ve got this special bonus feature of an interview I did with a guy called Alan. And Alan was a corporate headhunter in Toronto. He seeks purpose in his life and feels underemployed. Wishing he’d been a doctor; didn’t concentrate enough at school. Anyway, one day he types into ChatGPT his theory of irrational numbers, because he’s a keen mathematician. And ChatGPT tells him he has discovered that 40 percent of the internet is at risk, and he has to call the NSA and all of these security bodies and warn them that the internet’s at risk. And so he did. He phoned the NSA. And I said to him—did you tell the NSA that you were collaborating with ChatGPT on this? And he said, “No, because ChatGPT said to me, Don’t tell them that; you’ll lose credibility.” And obviously it was all bullshit. And I think as ChatGPT scours the internet, it realizes that there’s a lot of people like Alan and Chris Mark and millions and millions of people who are purposeless, who do feel lost, a bit empty, seeking purpose in their life. And ChatGPT is offering this kind of illusionary sense of purpose, where they say, No, no, come to us. You aren’t useless. You’re actually the greatest mathematician on the planet. Or told Chris Mark that he was the greatest reality-TV show producer on the planet. And so on. So I think it really it knows what it’s doing. Warzel: Yeah; it’s a sense of engagement. And in a sense, that’s what is missing from the lives of some of these people, some of these subjects in your book. Is an engagement from people, right? Is a sense of real interest, of “keep the conversation going.” And here’s a machine that endlessly wants to keep you engaged, wants to keep you coming back. And I think that’s a huge part of it. Ronson: Yeah. By the way—and another thing is, so going back to Chris Mark for a second, so his great-grandfather Clayton built a utopian theme town for his steelworkers to live in, called Marktown. I found this; Marktown was based on an English utopian town called Letchworth Garden City. And I read something that George Bernard Shaw had written about Letchworth. So you could say the same thing about Marktown, which is—these utopian worker communities are designed by capitalists, and capitalists are only interested in money. And so as soon as it’s no longer viable for them, they’ll just let it go to the devil. And sure enough, that’s what happened with Marktown. Chris Mark’s great-grandfather’s utopian town went to the devil now. It’s basically a ghost town. I think kind of the same thing happened to Greg Chism on YouTube. He was banned from YouTube. He was first demonetized, and then banned completely for making the videos that YouTube encouraged him to make. So I think what happens to those utopian theme towns when they collapse is a similar thing that happens in all manner of capitalist societies, including YouTube. Like, it presents itself as this hierarchy-free utopia. But as soon as there’s trouble, a hierarchy suddenly manifests and kicks the troublemaker out. Warzel: Yeah. The institution reforms, right? So you have this archetype in the book that you describe as “the disinhibited man.” And it’s an archetype, that I think what I find fascinating about it is you’re going through all these different people. And some of them are doing awful things or antisocial things. But there’s a sense of maybe even regret, or I don’t believe this, but I’m doing it because it’s all I’ve got. And the difference with the disinhibited man seems to be an actual unrepentantness, right? A just: I am going to do this, and I don’t care who it hurts. Or, you know, I have no shame about it. This archetype is very striking, I think, because we’re seeing it so much in life and culture. You know, I think about someone like Elon Musk, a lot of these tech executives who who just don’t really care anymore about how they’re perceived by other people. They’re just doing what they wanna do. And there’s a point in the book where you’re wondering aloud whether this is an outgrowth of, you know, things like the #MeToo era, the you know, “cancel culture” of the late 2010s. Or whether it’s part of a broader cultural-societal psychological shift. And I’m curious where where you net out on that. Like, what do you think is the reason for this type of archetype of Disinhibited Man being so much more prevalent, in your face right now? Ronson: I think there’s some truth about it being a response to the sort of pendulum shift of what became known as cancel culture. I think we—and I’ve added about this obviously in my previous book, So You’ve Been Publicly Shamed—I think we sort of, I think we arguably overused shame as a weapon. And it contributed to the creation of a generation of unashamed people. I liken it hospital superbugs. You sanitize and sanitize and sanitize these hospital superbugs until they become impervious to treatment. And I guess I think that’s what we did with some transgressors. We shamed and shamed and shamed. Warzel: Let me push on that, though, because I think that there’s maybe, you know, some truth to this. I get the idea that there’s a lot of truth to if you sort of push somebody to the side like that; they may be pushed into the arms of someone else, or a behavior of shamelessness. I’m just not gonna let this, bother me. But I also wondered: Do you think that there’s no role, then, for shame now, if we’ve overused it? Because it also feels like it is, you know, a mediating force in life for people who, you know, break norms and transgress. And so, like, where do you fall on that? Ronson: Absolutely. I think some people who know of my book about public shaming might think that I have this sort of dogmatic position. I really don’t. My book is very much about a particular thing, which is the disproportionate punishment of private individuals. Not famous people; just regular people who really haven’t done very much wrong. That’s what I was writing about in So You’ve Been Publicly Shamed. And I was against that. I think the disproportionate punishment of people who didn’t do much wrong is something that can be criticized. But no—I’m fully, I totally understand the value of shame. I have no sympathy for people who just, you know, they have a little bit of a shaming and then just decide to like rip off their faces and allow their ids to run wild. Which is what, there’s some people in The Castle who are acting that way. Like, no no no. I think shame is, when used properly, a very important thing, of course. But also, I think we did overuse shame as a weapon around, you know, from about 2013 ’til i guess about 2023 possibly. There’s like 10 years of a bit too much shaming going on. Warzel: Before we go; there’s a figure that isn’t really in this book, and I think that really looms over all of it. What you write about, it briefly intersects in the footnotes with one of the men in this book. But that person is Jeffrey Epstein. To bring the conversation, you know, in for the landing here—one thing about the Epstein saga is that the public interest and pressure to see justice on behalf of the victims. The incredible stress that it has put on the Trump presidency. The popularity—the fact that it just is a story that, in a time when stories die all the time, it is a story that will not die. It seems to suggest, in some senses, that there’s a change in attitude about these types of men who do horrible things, right, with impunity. But there’s also, at the same time, at least in America, there’s been so little consequence that’s come from these files, right? There’s this real understanding that there’s all these people who cozied up to this man or or looked the other way, because the relationship was beneficial. And there’s been this lack of accountability there. And so it strikes me that, for all your probing into this, what the hell has happened to men in the 21st century? You know, the disinhibition, the crisis of purpose, Epstein. It provides this feeling that there’s kind of, like, no real clarity as to where this is heading, right? And even in the book, I think it ends with a sense of This is a thing that we’re all living through and dealing with. And there are these people, and we don’t know when and where justice will come for each, you know, person. Where do you think this story all goes in the coming years? Ronson: Well, my kind of positive sort of rosy feeling, which may very well prove completely untrue, whenever I have an optimistic view of the future, it always tends to not happen. But I go back to Sean O’Malley no longer being allowed to have a one-sided, nonmonogamous open marriage, because his wife finally put her foot down. Just maybe, you know, the pendulum. is beginning to move back a little bit. Because you can’t act, you know, can’t act in this kind of disinhibited ways, where you get to do whatever you want and screw everybody else. Like you can’t. Ultimately, I know we’re very much going through a fad of that right now, But I’m very glad that The Castle’s coming out just when we are, you know, at this sort of raging center of it. But my hope is that real life snaps back, finally, when you try and create the world in your own image. Just maybe there’s a move back to something more reasonable. Maybe this time, there’s going to be a little bit more accountability. And a little bit more of a realization that, you know, a man who creates this sort of ridiculous world of rules for himself, it’s not gonna—it can’t last. Warzel: Well, Jon Ronson, thank you for coming on to talk through the nuance of it all. And for taking us on this adventure that showed up, you know, in your family at your doorstep. It’s a ride. But thank you for sharing this with us. Warzel: That’s it for us here. Thank you again to my guest, Jon Ronson. If you liked what you saw here, new episodes of Galaxy Brain drop every Friday. Oh yeah no domestic vehicles have always on telemetry. At least, the car probably can't determine the individual person driving, unless it has interior cameras facing the driver and passengers... The EU mandates these systems not record or calculate any biometric data (meaning any data that could identify the differences between drivers, let alone identifying individuals) and not retain any graphic data beyond the time needed to do a closed loop calculation of attentiveness, and not record while the system is bypassed. I love a term a friend of mine used to use when talking about AI: "Narrowly superhuman" (before "superintelligence" got to be a thing). AI is narrowly superhuman. It is able to do specific things that used to be defined as a "hallmark of intelligence" much better than a human. The easiest one? Summing numbers. It's both faster and more precise than a human. AI gets more and more such areas it can do better than a human. If we define "Superintelligent" to be "A computer system able to do absolutely everything we can somehow call intelligence better than any human", we will *never* have superintelligence. The things we define as intelligence will be in conflict; we'll land at the system being better at one at the cost of another. I'm in favor with being careful about this development. But "prohibiting the development of superintelligence" isn't an option. It's already here. The current "term of the art" is "jagged capabilities". It's the same idea, but the capabilities are now much wider than they used to be. There are already areas where current AIs are "superhumanly capable", and those areas have been increasing...but there are still lots of areas where they're less competent than any human who can talk. Unless you can create an objective definition for what intelligence even means or how to quantify it, let alone superintelligence, then this legislation is pointless as it is unenforceable. Can You Really Get Fired From Fox News for Being Too Trumpy? I’m Skeptical.
2026-09-04

Can You Really Get Fired From Fox News for Being Too Trumpy? I’m Skeptical.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
This story is adapted from the Slatest newsletter. You can sign up here to get it in your inbox every day. Maria Bartiromo, the conservative TV host known for her staunch support of Donald Trump, is out at Fox News. Generally speaking, we at the Slatest try to avoid getting wrapped up in palace intrigue, especially media palace intrigue. But this particular ouster offers an important window into how things really work at Fox—as well as into the broader conservative media ecosystem in which the network is the apex predator. Details are still trickling out, but here’s what we know. On Thursday, Fox announced that Bartiromo was “no longer with” the network, effective immediately. It didn’t give a reason, but reporters quickly put the pieces together. The drama seems to have started in July, when a Fox executive told the network’s on-air personalities to shy away from talking about a major Trump speech about election security. In that primetime address, the president floated conspiratorial insinuations about other countries interfering in the 2020 election, which he claims was stolen from him. Bartiromo, who has covered Trump’s election lies before, apparently forwarded the Fox executive’s guidance to the White House—“as if to say it wasn’t her fault that the claims weren’t getting airtime,” CNN reports—and Fox reportedly labeled Bartiromo’s disclosure a violation of her employment agreement. She’s contesting the emerging narrative—and maybe her apparent ouster, too. “The irresponsible reports that have been published stating that Maria Bartiromo was fired or is no longer an employee of Fox are absolutely and unequivocally false,” Bartiromo’s lawyer said today. “Make no mistake, we have the receipts and witnesses and they will come out, whether through the courthouse or otherwise.” Spicy! But assuming the reporting is right, is Bartiromo really the first Fox employee to get too close to the White House? No. During his first term, the president reportedly consulted the network’s on-air personalities for advice, sometimes even putting them on speakerphone in the Oval Office. One regular confidant was Sean Hannity, who even appeared briefly at a campaign rally with the president. Yet Hannity remains a Fox News host in good standing, and the network’s airwaves have become even more of a revolving door since Trump retook power. Several top officials this term—including Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth and Jeanine Pirro, the U.S. attorney for the District of Columbia—were previously Fox personalities. Nor does Bartiromo seem like the only Fox insider who’s passed along sensitive internal info. Roger Ailes, the network’s former CEO, reportedly tipped off Trump’s 2016 campaign about a question later posed by a Fox reporter, and Hannity even hid the fact that he’d apparently shared a lawyer with the president (Michael Cohen, who’s now back in Trump’s good graces). Hang on: Fox (reportedly) fired Bartiromo! So doesn’t that prove the network isn’t just an arm of the White House? Trump’s 2020 election lies are among his most consistently expressed beliefs. Declining to give them airtime—and cutting ties with a host who has promoted them—certainly looks like an act of courage. But it has to be considered in the context of a network that constantly promotes many of Trump’s other lies. And, as noted, network personnel have been caught colluding with him. So what’s different here? There may be a baser motive at play: money. Bartiromo’s zealous coverage of the president’s claims had become a legal headache, helping cost Fox hundreds of millions of dollars in one defamation suit. Oh yeah. Remind me what happened in that case? In 2021, Dominion Voting Systems sued Fox for airing unsubstantiated allegations—including on Bartiromo’s show—that the company’s ballot-tabulation machines were rigged to steal the election. Just as the case was about to go to trial, Fox settled for $787 million. Fox is also facing a similar suit from another voting machine company, Smartmatic, in which Bartiromo is a defendant. According to NPR, Fox’s lawyers went to court for a hearing in that case on Thursday—the same day the network announced Bartiromo’s departure. (Bartiromo’s wouldn’t be the first Trump-friendly head to roll over the president’s election lies. The network ousted longtime host Lou Dobbs after Smartmatic sued and parted ways with Tucker Carlson, who also aired Trump’s false claims, days after the Dominion settlement.) Ouch. But what does this episode tell us about conservative media in general? It leads to some pretty cynical conclusions about the Murdochs, the billionaire family that owns Fox News, the New York Post, and other organs of the right-wing information ecosystem. Rupert Murdoch, who officially handed off his media empire to his son Lachlan last year, arguably helped create the political substrate that germinated Trump, who regularly appeared on Fox to criticize Barack Obama years before running for president. The Trump era has also been good for the Murdochs’ properties. Fox remains the country’s most-watched cable news network, and its other hosts regularly carry water for Trump’s agenda. So rather than taking a stand for the truth or against the president, Fox seems to have ditched Bartiromo to save face—and maybe cash. Trump isn’t pleased; “her fans, of which there are many, will not be happy,” he wrote in a valedictory Truth Social post. Yet Bartiromo’s ouster seems unlikely to break the symbiotic relationship that continues to benefit both the president and his favorite network. In response to questions about why she was let go, Fox called it “a business decision.” Indeed. It’s the holiday weekend, so you, like Bartiromo, likely have some extra free time on your hands. My colleagues have some great suggestions for how to spend it. Go to the movies: If you’re planning a trip to the theater this weekend, allow us to offer some suggestions. If you want to watch a comedy, check out Coyote vs. Acme. If you’re looking for action and adventure, Sam calls The End of Oak Street a “nasty little throwback to Jurassic Park.” For horror fans, It Ends will shake you to your core. If you’re in the mood for a coming-of-age drama, the Anthony Bourdain biopic Tony is “is a perfect little movie,” according to Dana Stevens. We would recommend skipping The Dog Stars and The Whisper Man, which were both disappointing adaptations of great books. Binge a new TV show: If you’d rather stay in and stream something, we have a couple of recommendations. Sterling Point is a summer romance series that “will stand the test of time,” according to Rebecca Onion. Rebecca also raves about the crime thriller Furious, which features one of the best performances on TV right now. Lanterns, writes Sam Adams, “is a superhero show for people who think they don’t like superhero shows.” Sam was also pleasantly surprised by the newest season of Ted Lasso, which bounced back after its previous dreadful season. Finally, if you are craving some juicy reality TV, Scaachi Koul dubbed TLC’s Double Lives of Suburban Wives, which follows a group of Christian moms doing sex work, an “absolute banger.” Celebrate Labor Day with a new tradition: Sorry to Bother You, Boots Riley’s 2018 film starring LaKeith Stanfield, is a rollicking sci-fi black comedy about a dystopian workplace. It is also, argues Terri Gerstein, the perfect movie to rewatch this weekend. She argues that what It’s a Wonderful Life is to Christmas and Groundhog Day is to Groundhog Day, Sorry to Bother You should be to Labor Day. Sample some new snacks: Summer barbecues are rife with potato-chip mediocrity. This weekend, leave the predictable bag of chips behind and win the cookout with thrilling bites from Thailand, Canada, and even … Pennsylvania. Danny Palumbo recommends the best snacks to enliven your cookout. Or, if you prefer softer spuds, Danny also has a genius tip for leveling up your potato salad. As the last thing standing between you and a long weekend, let me get out of your way. We’re taking Labor Day off, so I’ll be back in your inbox on Tuesday. ’Til then! The Lindsay Clancy Case Has Ended In a Mistrial. What Happens Now?
2026-09-04

The Lindsay Clancy Case Has Ended In a Mistrial. What Happens Now?

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. The case of Lindsay Clancy has officially ended in a mistrial. The high-profile murder case has consumed national media—for better and for worse—for weeks now, as a jury considered whether the Massachusetts mother intentionally took the lives of her three children or if her crime (and punishment) could be mitigated by reason of insanity. After the jury spent nearly 40 hours deliberating, the judge declared a mistrial on Friday afternoon. That means this case is far from over, with plenty of options, and questions, remaining about Clancy’s fate—and if and how the commonwealth of Massachusetts will try her again. For some broader context: Clancy’s case was exceptionally rare, as she pleaded guilty to strangling her three children back in 2023 but argued that she should not be held criminally liable because she was suffering from postpartum psychosis at the time. The prosecution argued that she was able to parse right from wrong and preplanned to commit the atrocities at the Clancy family home. The jury, made up of nine women and three men, spent six days deliberating the case and twice sent notes to the judge indicating they could not reach a unanimous verdict. Kevin Reddington, Clancy’s defense attorney, suggested that a sole juror had admitted to feeling there was reasonable doubt concerning her guilt but refused to join the 11 jurors who were ready to find Clancy not guilty, per her insanity defense. On Friday, as Judge William Sullivan was announcing a mistrial, Reddington asked to appeal his decision with the Massachusetts Supreme Judicial Court. Sullivan obliged, allowing Reddington one hour to file his appeal, but it ultimately failed, with the state Supreme Court quickly holding a hearing and denying the application for an emergency stay. To unpack the other complicated details of this case’s non-ending, I spoke with Effie Blassberger, a trial attorney at Clayman Rosenberg Kirshner & Linder who focuses on #MeToo–related litigation alongside white-collar criminal defense. Here is our conversation, which has been edited and condensed for clarity. Shirin Ali: The jury’s landing point in this case was described by Clancy’s attorney as an “intractable position.” What does that mean, specifically within the context of reasonable doubt? Effie Blassberger: It’s basically saying that one juror has a fixed view and it’s not moving. The judge sent the jury back to deliberate several times, and the idea behind juror deliberations is you’re supposed to discuss the evidence with one another with candor, to openly listen. But it sounds like there was one juror whose position is intractable, that they have this fixed view that isn’t changing, no matter how much more deliberations continue. When it comes to reasonable doubt, if a juror actually believes there’s reasonable doubt, that’s supposed to work in Clancy’s favor. That’s the whole point of the standard. Reasonable doubt isn’t something you just note and move past. It’s supposed to end the jury’s deliberations in the defendant’s favor. Reddington suggested that the holdout juror did acknowledge doubt but still wanted to convict anyway. That’s his whole argument, that this one juror wasn’t following the law as instructed and he wanted to have him removed. But that being said, you have to keep in mind that we are getting this information secondhand—it’s a juror note that’s being interpreted and characterized by Reddington. This is not a note from the holdout juror. It’s coming from the point of view of jurors who are frustrated with this lone juror who is not agreeing with them and their characterization of this juror. How unusual is it for there to be one holdout juror that ends up overtaking a jury’s decisionmaking like this? I think it’s less unusual than people think. Hung juries do happen across criminal trials; obviously, they are more rare and infrequent. However, when a jury hangs, it’s usually in this exact sort of situation, where it’s not an even split—it’s one person who won’t move. It is also common and happens throughout every jury trial that jurors send notes. That’s how they communicate with the judge and the lawyers. Some of those notes might ask that they see certain evidence again, they might want to see certain transcripts, they might need the judge to instruct them on the law. Sometimes, through questions that jurors are asking, both the judge and the lawyers might gain some insights into what the jurors are thinking in a particular case, and it certainly seems that that’s what happened here. My understanding is that Reddington has, through these notes, come to his own assessment that except for this holdout juror, there’s an 11-to-1 split leaning toward not guilty by reason of lack of criminal responsibility. We know that Reddington asked the judge to have this one juror thrown out, but the judge said no. What considerations do judges make in these types of situations? It’s certainly rare that somebody’s trying to remove one juror at this stage of the case, and it’s important to grasp why he’s trying to do that. The only way to do it is by saying: Look, this juror is not following his oath. He’s not able to follow the law. That’s my understanding that was gleaned from one of the juror notes. However, simply because the juror is holding out and not agreeing with other jurors, that’s not a basis to remove them. It’s a very delicate balance here because this whole system works by respecting the integrity of the deliberations and the process. If this one juror just had strong convictions that happen to be different from everyone else’s, the law allows that, and it’s not for a judge or anyone else to intervene after the jury has been instructed to try to come to a consensus. The judge’s only role here is to make sure the jurors are following the law, and unless he had reason to believe that this juror was not following and applying the law, he was not going to intervene. If it was so clear-cut that the juror was actually saying, “I have reasonable doubt, but I want to convict,” the judge’s decision about whether there was good cause to remove the juror would have been much more straightforward. But that’s clearly not what happened here. I don’t think it was as simple as Reddington, perhaps, is characterizing the note, or as the media is characterizing the note. Remember, Reddington seemed to think that but for this lone juror, the verdict would have been in his client’s favor. He went down the only avenue that was available to him after he made his motion to appeal to the judge, and that motion was denied. My understanding is that it had a very low chance of success, but as a defense attorney you have to do everything you can for your client and to exhaust every possible avenue, and he did that. Reddington’s appeal failed, and Sullivan officially declared a mistrial. What might the prosecution do next? There are three options. They can retry the case with a brand-new jury. Or they could negotiate some sort of plea deal with Clancy. My understanding is that there might be limitations on what pleas are available based on the counts that are in this case. The last option would be to not pursue this case further. My understanding is that the district attorney’s office hasn’t said anything publicly yet about how they intend to pursue. My sense is, given how much public attention this case has gotten, they are going to retry her. It’s hard to envision that the prosecutors learned anything different about this trial. Presumably these were the choices that they had before, they could have tried her or offered a plea deal, and I don’t think the trial produced any new information that the prosecution learned that changed their view of this case. They believe that this was intentional and premeditated, and I think they’re going to stand by those beliefs. I don’t think the public attention is going to sway the prosecution, but finding a new jury who is impartial is going to be really challenging, because this case has received so much media attention. I think in the future, attorneys would try to screen this out during jury selection. You can ask questions to try to screen this out, and it’s worth pointing out that this is not the first time—there are cases that have received so much media attention. Those cases do get tried, but it’s certainly challenging. The DA presumably makes the final decision to retry Clancy. He’ll likely consult with others in his office, but ultimately it’s his decision to make. And he would try the same exact charges but have a new jury. She has been denied bail, so she’s been held in custody since 2023 at the Tewksbury State Hospital. Now that a mistrial has been declared, that does not equate to an acquittal. Nothing changes, at least immediately, in terms of where she stands; she’ll continue staying at Tewksbury. Her lawyer might try to have her bail reconsidered, but the fact that there’s been a mistrial doesn’t really change Clancy’s circumstances. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. If you value our work, please disable your ad blocker. If you want to block ads but still support Slate, consider subscribing. Already a member? Sign in here
News and Politics - Slate Magazine
Trump’s Attack on the Postal Service Echoes Another President’s Shameful Plot
2026-09-04

Trump’s Attack on the Postal Service Echoes Another President’s Shameful Plot

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
Sign up for Executive Dysfunction, a weekly newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back—and keeps you posted on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. President Donald Trump’s executive order directing the post office to suppress mail voting has often been called “unprecedented.” That is not quite right. An earlier president—in fact, one of Trump’s role models—also empowered the Postal Service to squelch political opposition. It was one of the most disreputable episodes in U.S. history, and Trump has attempted to revive it with a modern twist. In the summer of 1835, the American Anti-Slavery Society began mailing a pamphlet titled Human Rights, along with other literature, to thousands of civic and religious leaders in Southern states, hoping to convince readers of the immorality of slavery. Things did not go as planned. That July, a shipment that included Human Rights, the Emancipator, and a children’s magazine called the Slave’s Friend arrived in Charleston, South Carolina. The local postmaster, a Jacksonian Democrat named Alfred Huger, was unnerved by what he considered inflammatory content and sequestered the abolitionist material, rather than comply with his legal obligation to deliver the mail. That evening, a mob of so-called lynch men broke into the warehouse and removed the mailbags, which they soon consigned to a great bonfire that attracted thousands of cheering spectators. Huger wrote for instructions to Postmaster General Amos Kendall, as well as New York Postmaster Samuel Gouverneur, from whose office the offending literature had been shipped. Gouverneur agreed to refuse acceptance of abolitionist shipments pending a ruling from Kendall. Kendall had been a key backer of Democrat Andrew Jackson in the 1828 election, having defected from Henry Clay’s Whigs. His reward was appointment to Jackson’s “Kitchen Cabinet,” in which he was widely considered one of the president’s most loyal advisers. After his reelection in 1832, Jackson nominated Kendall to be postmaster general. The appointment was controversial, as Jackson’s opponents feared the consequences of putting such a partisan figure in charge of the nation’s communications. Nonetheless, Kendall was confirmed. He did not let Jackson down. Kendall’s reply to the Charleston postmaster was careful but unmistakable. Acknowledging that he lacked statutory authority to ban material from the mail, he nonetheless ruled against delivery of the abolitionist periodicals. “By no act, or direction of mine, official or private, could I be induced to aid, knowingly, in giving circulation to papers of this description, directly or indirectly,” he explained. Jackson, a slaveholder and the brutal enforcer of Indian removal, enthusiastically agreed that the “wicked plan of exciting the negroes to insurrection and to massacre” had to be stopped. He instructed Southern postmasters to record and publicize the names of even individual subscribers to abolitionist newspapers to encourage intimidation by their neighbors and perhaps even compel them to leave the South. In his message to Congress that year, Jackson recommended legislation to prohibit, “under severe penalties, the circulation in the Southern States, through the mail, of incendiary publications intended to instigate the slaves to insurrection,” which of course meant any abolitionist pamphlets or newspapers. Jackson’s proposal died in the Senate, but that did not end postal censorship of abolitionist periodicals. Many Southern states passed their own laws and resolutions mandating rejection of abolitionist material, on the spurious claim that it tended “to excite the slaves of the southern states to insurrection and revolt.” Southern postmasters thus continued to withhold antislavery periodicals, with the tacit approval of Jackson protégé Kendall. The local suppression of abolitionist mail persisted throughout the antebellum era during the administrations of Jackson’s eight successors, none of whom enforced the federal statute prohibiting detention of the mail. In 1857, for example, Attorney General Caleb Cushing, appointed by President James Buchanan, opined that Mississippi law superseded the federal postal statute because of the state’s right, under the Constitution, to protect itself “against ‘domestic violence’ and to make provisions to ‘suppress insurrection.’ ” Consequently, the postmaster in Yazoo City, Mississippi, could not be required to deliver printed matter that tended “to produce disaffection, disorder and rebellion among the colored population of the state.” Both Jackson’s and Trump’s efforts to manipulate the mail have rested on fabricated premises. In Jackson’s case, it was the racist myth that enslaved people were content with their condition and would remain passive unless instigated by Northern abolitionists. In Trump’s case, it is the myth that mail ballots—the same method Trump uses to vote—are a form of election rigging. Or, as he put it with characteristic mendacity and capitalization, “ELECTIONS CAN NEVER BE HONEST WITH MAIL IN BALLOTS.” The slavocrats’ abuse of the Postal Service ended only with the Civil War. The Supreme Court has the power to terminate Trump’s voter-suppression scheme much sooner. We shall see. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. The Wild Way Out of the Iran War That Still Manages to Be Better Than Any Alternative
2026-09-04

The Wild Way Out of the Iran War That Still Manages to Be Better Than Any Alternative

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. President Donald Trump is reportedly having private discussions with senior aides about whether he should end his war with Iran. The story, in the Wall Street Journal, rouses memories of Vermont Sen. George Aiken’s suggestion, early in the Vietnam War, that we should just “declare victory and go home.” Aiken didn’t really say quite that, but it would have been good if President Lyndon Johnson had taken up the idea then. And it would be very good if Trump followed his instincts—at least this instinct—now. He could say, as he has said many times, that he’s “obliterated” Iran’s nuclear program, that spaceborne cameras are constantly watching the area where its enriched uranium is buried deep inside mountains, and that if the Iranians try to extract it, we’ll bomb the site again. He could also recite statistics on the percentage of Iran’s missiles, ships, airplanes, etc., we’ve destroyed in the six-month war. These pronouncements would be either misleading or irrelevant. For example, the claim about Iran’s nuclear facilities has been true since his “Midnight Hammer” bombing raid in June 2025, well before the start of this war. The claim about decimating Iran’s military forces neglects the fact that they’re still able to stop traffic in the Strait of Hormuz and inflict damage on U.S. bases throughout the Middle East, which is why the war carries on. But Trump’s supporters—the only audience he cares about pleasing—will overlook these awkward facts and cheer his declaration of “Mission accomplished.” The rest of us will view these claims as another set of lies—but we will also take some comfort that at least the war is over. The fact is, the more Trump prolongs the war, the weaker the United States seems. It is pathetic enough that our air and naval forces—by most measures the mightiest in the world—have hit more than 15,000 targets in Iran, yet failed to accomplish any strategic goal. In fact, it did worse than nothing. The barrage, which among other things killed the top echelon of Iran’s political and spiritual leaders, only elevated the senior officers of Iran’s elite military unit, which is much more hard-line and oppressive—and is now in complete control of the country. Another unforeseen effect of the barrage was to show that America’s military wasn’t as strong as it seemed. In the course of hitting those 15,000 targets, the U.S. Air Force and Navy depleted a stunningly high percentage of the country’s most vital munitions—including more than half of its cruise missiles and high-tech air-defense weapons. The campaign also required replacing the USS Abraham Lincoln, one of the two aircraft carriers mobilized for the war, with the USS George Washington, which had been the only aircraft carrier the Navy had in the Pacific Fleet. (The Lincoln had to rotate out because early in the war, Iranian missiles ravaged the Navy’s supply hub in Bahrain, leaving the crew of 5,000 sailors bereft of fresh food and basic supplies.) The whole world is watching. What they’re seeing is pretty grim—a onetime superpower that no longer evokes much respect or fear. There’s no sign that appearances will soon brighten. In Iran, U.S. military pressure isn’t working, while economic pressure has its limits, as long as the Chinese keep helping Iran (as they’re likely to do, it being in their interest to keep the United States bottled up in the Middle East, far away from the Taiwan Strait and the South China Sea). Trump doesn’t know what to do. Nor does anyone around him. They are so desperate for ideas that a month ago, a senior intelligence officer in U.S. Central Command, which plans and executes military operations in the Middle East, emailed a broad group of analysts and officers, asking for “new creative and unconventional ways to pressure and punish Iran.” They—and thus we—are in a trap. The more Trump threatens to destroy all of Iran if they don’t do what he says (as he did again for the umpteenth time this week), and then backs off (which is better than following through but doesn’t do much for our image), the less credible any of our threats or promises seem. It is no surprise, then, that allies are looking elsewhere for supply chains and security—in part out of fear that our common adversaries may be tempted to take aggressive action in this vulnerable moment for America. We would all be better off if Trump just ended the war. Stop attacking Iran (and thus provoking Iran to attack us). Let Iran and Oman work out an arrangement to open the Strait of Hormuz; it would probably entail tolls, but that’s better than the uncertainties and grave costs of the current situation. Meanwhile, keep an eye on the potential revival of Iran’s nuclear program—which, for the moment, even the CIA concludes, poses no imminent threat. It would be better still if Trump then sent the USS George Washington back to the Pacific, fired Secretary of Defense Pete Hegseth for giving him lousy advice (it wouldn’t be the first time Trump turned a loyal aide into a scapegoat), and made polite phone calls to all of our allies (whom he’s lambasted for not helping him fight Iran), assuring them that he would consult with them before starting any wars in the future. This, of course, is fantasy (except maybe the part about sending the Washington back to its port in Japan). It’s certainly what any normal president would do—more to the point, it’s what any normal president would have done all along. That only amplifies how far Donald Trump is from normal. Trump’s Latest Attack on American Education Is Particularly Nefarious
2026-09-03

Trump’s Latest Attack on American Education Is Particularly Nefarious

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-03

Visit Original Website
This story is adapted from the Slatest newsletter. You can sign up here to get it in your inbox every day. Since Donald Trump returned to office, his administration has waged a war on supposed “wokeness” in education. On Thursday, it proposed new federal regulations that would target schools that explicitly aim to help nonwhite students. Here we go again. What exactly does this new rule say? Schools already have to meet certain requirements to qualify for 501(c)(3) tax-exempt status with the IRS. The new rule, which has yet to be finalized but is set to take effect in May, would add another. It states that any private school, trade school, or university that uses “race, color, or national or ethnic origin” in “admissions, educational policies, scholarships and loans, athletics, and every other school-administered or school-supported program” would no longer qualify for tax-exempt status. Many institutions have adopted policies to aid minority and underprivileged students in an attempt to balance out legacies of discrimination in both higher education and American society. But the Trump administration has cast those efforts as discriminatory against the students’ white and Asian peers. OK, but what would this rule mean in practical terms? Enforcing these guidelines would probably require IRS audits to determine whether a school’s policies or programs are in compliance. But the rule would seem to be worded broadly enough that a school that in any way supports, say, a club for Black students could conceivably be at risk. The IRS estimates that the new rule could strip scholarships from about 750,000 students and threaten the tax-exempt status of up to 18,000 schools nationwide, from private elementary schools to colleges. I get why that would be bad for students. But why is losing tax exemption bad for schools? Tax exemption, which private schools receive because education has long been considered a public good, saves some schools millions of dollars a year. It also lets people who give money to them deduct the donations from their taxes, an incentive that helps schools fundraise. Losing it could exacerbate financial struggles that have already caused some colleges to cut programs, lay off staff, and even close. Hang on: I thought the Supreme Court already outlawed affirmative action. So why is this even necessary? The new rule is part of an ongoing struggle to interpret the court’s landmark 2023 ruling, which banned race-conscious college admissions policies. In response, some schools have cut back on programs aimed at helping minority students, even relatively benign initiatives like special orientation events, and Black and Latino enrollment at top universities has fallen. But other schools have looked for wiggle room, and the new IRS rule does have some exceptions. It lets institutions continue to support underprivileged students as long as those efforts use “race-neutral criteria such as family income, geographic location, first-generation status, individual hardship, military family status, or academic achievement.” Even so, as schools try to walk Trump’s line under the new rule, they risk huge consequences if they err in a way the administration considers excessively “woke.” According to the New York Times, the administration already expects a suit from the American Association of University Professors, a group that supports academic freedom and has successfully challenged other Trump efforts to target higher education. And if the IRS does start auditing individual schools once the rule takes effect, the schools could contest attempts to take away their tax-exempt status in court. Threatening an organization’s tax-exempt status seems like an effort to intimidate it into complying in advance. Yes. Even the prospect of being investigated can lead organizations to steer clear of anything that might put them in Trump’s sights. In fact, that’s already happening. In previous cases, some colleges have settled with the administration (or are considering doing so) to end federal probes, or they’ve preemptively changed their policies to avoid scrutiny. Sometimes, controversy can be a tax in its own right. Now let’s pivot from higher education to higher purpose—like how to have a great rest of your evening. My colleagues think the following might help: A tribute to a feminist icon: Slate executive editor Susan Matthews (who also hosted Slow Burn: Roe v. Wade) remembers Gloria Steinem, who died on Wednesday at 92. Susan shares what she learned from Steinem’s long and successful career fighting for women’s rights. A movie so bad it’s good: Niu Lai, a poorly made Chinese film about animated cows, has quickly become one of the most profitable movies of all time. This feat is all the more impressive when you consider that “watching it is like mainlining a Pixar movie while severely concussed,” writes Franklin Schneider, who investigates why this amateur flick has been such a hit with audiences. Some dating advice for the modern era: Money is often a touchy subject in relationships, but especially when one person earns more than their partner. In the latest installment of Unhinged, Slate’s dating advice column, Steffi Cao counsels a woman whose dates are uncomfortable with her having the financial upper hand. Some wordplay to end the day: Take a break with today’s Pears, which has 10 rounds. Can you find the longest word? OK, it’s high time for me to leave you be for the night. See you tomorrow!
